**Campaign**: "3KM Radius | Search | Brand | Nudge" (ID 23238305704), account 2203472299, currency AUD.

**Root cause — reconstructed from the Google Ads change history:**

At 21:22:26 (web client, your edit), the campaign's Target Impression Share bid strategy had its CPC bid ceiling raised from A$1.50 to A$2.50 — this is the cap loosening you intended, and it's a normal parameter tweak that does not by itself reset learning.

At 21:33:08 — 11 minutes later — a separate change came in via the Google Ads API that set a `target_spend` (Maximize Clicks) bid-ceiling value of A$2.50 on the same campaign. Setting a value on a different bidding-strategy sub-object like this represents a **bidding strategy switch away from Target Impression Share**, and a bidding-strategy-type change is what actually forces the campaign back into a learning phase — not the cap edit itself.

```json
{"table": {"title": "Change History — Campaign 23238305704 (2026-07-21)", "headers": ["Time", "Source", "Change", "Old Value", "New Value"], "rows": [["21:22:26", "Web (your edit)", "Target Impression Share — CPC bid ceiling", "A$1.50", "A$2.50"], ["21:33:08", "API (automated)", "Target Spend bid ceiling introduced (strategy touch)", "— (not set)", "A$2.50"]]}}
```

**Current live state of the campaign** (checked just now): the bidding strategy is back on **Target Impression Share** (Top of Page, 95% target share) — so the accidental strategy touch is not the active strategy. However, the CPC bid ceiling inside Target Impression Share is currently sitting at **A$1.50**, i.e. your intended cap loosening to A$2.50 did not persist — it got wiped out along with the accidental strategy touch.

**What "back the way it should be, without undoing the cap change" requires**: no bidding-strategy-type change at all (it's already correctly on Target Impression Share) — just re-apply the CPC bid ceiling to A$2.50 within the existing Target Impression Share strategy (leaving Top of Page / 95% location target untouched).

**I could not execute this automatically**: the available Google Ads campaign-update tool's bidding strategy field only supports MANUAL_CPC, TARGET_CPA, TARGET_ROAS, MAXIMIZE_CONVERSIONS, MAXIMIZE_CONVERSION_VALUE, MANUAL_CPV, TARGET_CPV, TARGET_SPEND/MAXIMIZE_CLICKS — Target Impression Share is not one of the editable types exposed by this tool. Forcing the change through TARGET_SPEND would recreate the exact accidental strategy switch that caused the learning reset, so I did not do that.

**Recommended next step**: apply the CPC bid ceiling change (A$1.50 → A$2.50) directly in the Google Ads UI under this campaign's bidding settings while it remains on Target Impression Share — no other setting needs to change. If you'd like, I can re-check the tool for Target Impression Share support periodically, or you can confirm once it's applied and I'll verify the change stuck.

**Bottom line: The account-level CTR increase is a traffic-mix artifact, not a genuine performance improvement.** Based on Google Ads data for account 4546011805, comparing 2026-06-19–2026-07-05 ("Period 1") vs 2026-07-06–2026-07-22 ("Period 2"):

- Blended account CTR: 0.782% → 0.948% (+21.2% relative)
- But **4 of 5 campaigns saw CTR flat-to-declining**, and the one meaningful volume campaign with a real (small) CTR gain only moved 5.1%→5.7% of impression share.

```json
{"table": {"title": "Campaign-Level CTR: Period 1 vs Period 2", "headers": ["Campaign", "Channel", "P1 Impr.", "P1 CTR", "P2 Impr.", "P2 CTR", "CTR Δ", "Impr. Share P1→P2"], "rows": [["Bluefunnel_YT_RT_v2", "Video", "18,672", "0.257%", "21,180", "0.165%", "-35.7%", "88.0% → 71.0%"], ["Jul26_Conversion_Search_NonBrand_US", "Search", "1,092", "7.967%", "1,695", "8.260%", "+3.7%", "5.1% → 5.7%"], ["Jul26_Competitor_DirectSwitchers_US", "Search", "556", "2.698%", "2,744", "2.150%", "-20.3%", "2.6% → 9.2%"], ["Jul26_Competitor_JobSearchTools_US", "Search", "862", "1.276%", "3,674", "0.953%", "-25.3%", "4.1% → 12.3%"], ["Jul26_Competitor_Telugu_US", "Search", "40", "12.500%", "554", "2.527%", "-79.8%", "0.2% → 1.9%"]]}}
```

**What actually happened**: The Video campaign ("Bluefunnel_YT_RT_v2") — which runs at a structurally low ~0.2% CTR — dominated impression volume in Period 1 (88% share) but lost share in Period 2 (down to 71%) as the four Search campaigns roughly tripled their combined impression volume (from ~2,550 to ~8,667). Since Search inherently converts far more impressions into clicks than a Video/remarketing placement, this reallocation mechanically pulls the blended average CTR upward — even while every individual campaign except one got *worse* or stayed flat at the click-through level.

I ran a formal decomposition (holding Period-1 impression mix fixed to isolate the "rate effect" from the "mix effect"):

```json
{"chart": {"type": "bar", "title": "CTR Change Decomposition: Mix Shift vs Actual Rate Change", "data": {"labels": ["Rate Effect (real CTR change)", "Mix Effect (traffic reallocation)", "Total Observed Change"], "datasets": [{"label": "CTR change (percentage points)", "data": [-0.112, 0.278, 0.166]}]}}}
```

**The rate effect is negative (-0.112 pp)** — meaning if the traffic mix had stayed identical to Period 1, blended CTR would have *fallen*, not risen. The **mix effect is positive (+0.278 pp)** and large enough to flip the overall number positive (+0.166 pp net). In plain terms: campaigns individually got less clickable; the account only looks better because more impressions shifted toward inherently-higher-CTR Search inventory and away from the low-CTR Video placement.

A few caveats on confidence: click volumes are thin across the Search campaigns (11–140 clicks per period), and conversions were 0 in Period 1 and only 2 in Period 2 account-wide — well below the volume needed for statistically reliable conclusions on any single campaign's trend (the Telugu campaign's -79.8% CTR swing, for example, is based on just 40 impressions in Period 1). Currency code for this account could not be confirmed via the API in this session, so cost figures above are reported in the account's native reporting currency as returned by the API, unlabeled.

**Practical implication**: don't treat the improved account CTR as validation of creative or targeting changes. The real signal is that the Video campaign's engagement rate is deteriorating (-35.7%) even as it still carries ~71% of impression volume, and two of the Competitor Search campaigns (DirectSwitchers, JobSearchTools) grew impression share 3x while their CTR fell 20–25%, with cost roughly tripling for near-zero conversion return in this window.<creditUsed>6.777355200000001</creditUsed>

The visible budget cells were overstated primarily because Meta's `daily_budget` field is returned in **cents**, not dollars (e.g., "4700" = $47.00, not $4,700), and a naive rollup would also double-count campaign-level (CBO) budgets against ad-set-level (ABO) budgets, and include paused campaigns that cannot spend. Correcting for all three, here is what each of the nine rows can actually spend per day:

```json
{"table": {"title": "Actual Daily Spend Capacity — 9 Client Rows", "headers": ["Client Row", "Account", "Platform", "Actual $/Day", "Basis"], "rows": [
["Creative Outdoor Solutions", "8267821813", "Google Ads", "$222.00", "6 ENABLED campaigns, distinct (non-shared) budgets"],
["Frerichs", "5284425541", "Google Ads", "$40.00", "2 ENABLED campaigns (MLD-RAL-Locals $30 + MLD-RAL-Tourists $10)"],
["Sauna House", "act_775281912070002", "Meta", "$47.00", "1 ACTIVE CBO campaign (4700¢→$47); 1 PAUSED campaign excluded (no live budget)"],
["McKibbon — Bargello", "act_2249164298612104", "Meta", "$12.00", "CBO campaign only (1200¢)"],
["McKibbon — Capella", "act_2249164298612104", "Meta", "$9.00", "CBO campaign $7 + shared newsletter ad set $2"],
["McKibbon — Nuvole", "act_2249164298612104", "Meta", "$8.00", "CBO campaign $6 + shared newsletter ad set $2"],
["McKibbon — Urban Oak", "act_2249164298612104", "Meta", "$17.00", "CBO campaign $15 + shared newsletter ad set $2"],
["McKibbon — Waving Girl", "act_2249164298612104", "Meta", "$60.00", "CBO campaign only (6000¢); see unmapped note below"],
["McKibbon — Wildwood", "act_2249164298612104", "Meta", "$18.00", "ABO campaign, 2 live ad sets ($6+$10) + shared newsletter ad set $2. Excludes a PAUSED $16/day 'Reach' campaign that cannot spend"]
]}}
```

**Total actually spendable across the 9 rows: $447.00/day** ($222 Creative Outdoor Solutions + $40 Frerichs + $47 Sauna House + $124 McKibbon core + $14 unmapped McKibbon item, detailed below).

Three specific gaps explain why the raw cells looked inflated:

- **Cents-as-dollars misread (Meta only):** Every McKibbon and Sauna House campaign/ad-set budget is stored in cents by the Meta API. If a rollup displayed the raw field without dividing by 100, every Meta row would appear 100x too high (e.g., Bargello showing "$1,200" instead of $12.00).
- **Paused budgets still counted:** McKibbon's "MLD-WildWood-2026-Reach" campaign (PAUSED) carries a $16.00/day budget that cannot spend today — including it would overstate Wildwood by $16/day. Sauna House's paused "MLD-RAL-Traffic-Pre_Sale" campaign carries no live budget at all.
- **CBO vs. ABO mixing:** Properties like Capella, Nuvole, Urban Oak, and Wildwood are funded partly by their own campaign-level (CBO) budget and partly by a shared ad set inside "MLD-All_Locations-2026-Newsletter-Leads" (ABO, $2.00/day per property ad set). A rollup that only reads campaign-level `daily_budget` and ignores ad-set-level budgets would under-count these four; one that double-adds both without checking CBO/ABO status would over-count them.

One unresolved item: an ACTIVE Meta campaign named "Instagram post: Girl Dinner just got a room..." in the McKibbon account carries a $14.00/day budget but doesn't follow the "MLD-{Property}" naming convention used everywhere else, so I did not attribute it to any of the six named properties — it's a standalone boosted post at the account level. If it belongs to Waving Girl or another property, that row would need a manual +$14.00/day adjustment.

All Google Ads figures come directly from `campaign_budget.amount_micros` already converted to account currency (USD) by the account structure — no unit-conversion issue was found on the Google Ads side, and no shared budgets are in use across either Google Ads account.

```json
{"table": {"title": "Search Conversion Traceability Reconciliation (2026-05-25 to 2026-06-23)", "headers": ["Level", "Conversions Traced", "% of Total (290.97)", "Notes"], "rows": [["Campaign total (reported)", "290.97", "100.0%", "Source of truth"], ["Tied to a specific keyword criterion", "288.97", "99.3%", "34 distinct converting keywords, ALL currently status = ENABLED"], ["NOT tied to any keyword row", "2.00", "0.7%", "Residual gap — doesn't reconcile to any individual keyword_view row"], ["Tied to a visible, literal search term string", "177.47", "61.0%", "151 distinct search terms captured"], ["NOT tied to a visible search term (withheld by Google)", "113.50", "39.0%", "Google's privacy/low-volume threshold hides these queries entirely"]]}}
```

**What this means for finance, broken down:**

1. **Keyword linkage is strong.** 99.3% of conversions trace to one of 34 converting keywords, and every one of those keywords currently shows status ENABLED — no paused or removed keyword is currently holding attributed conversion credit. A small residual (~2.0 conversions, 0.7%) doesn't reconcile to any individual keyword row; this is a minor gap worth flagging but not material.

2. **Search-term linkage is materially weaker.** Only 61% of conversions map to a literal, visible search query (e.g., "headhunter," "recolocação profissional," "job hunter sao paulo"). The remaining 39% (113.5 conversions) cannot be tied to an actual user search string — this is not a tracking failure on your end; Google Ads structurally withholds a portion of search terms from the Search Terms report for privacy/low-query-volume reasons, and those clicks/conversions never surface as an identifiable query. This is a permanent, by-design gap, not something fixable via account configuration.

3. **Some "identified" conversions are modeled, not deterministic.** Several rows carry fractional conversion counts (0.5, 0.989272, 0.994172, 1.5, etc.) rather than whole numbers — for example the SP campaign shows 128.988658 total conversions. This is the signature of Google's data-driven/probabilistic attribution splitting credit across multiple touchpoints or devices. Even where a keyword and search term ARE identified, the "1 click = 1 conversion" assumption doesn't hold for all of them.

4. **One check was inconclusive due to a tool limitation.** I attempted to verify (a) whether any ad groups in these campaigns are Dynamic Search Ads (which serve without keywords) and (b) whether any paused/removed keywords hold conversions outside this window. The status/type filters on these specific fields returned unfiltered result sets in this session rather than honoring the filter, so I can't make a reliable claim either way. If finance needs airtight assurance on this specific point, I'd confirm directly in the Google Ads UI (Ad Groups list → Type column, and Keywords tab filtered by status) rather than rely on this session's query output.

**Bottom line for finance:** the keyword-to-status linkage is clean (no dead/paused keywords collecting credit), but roughly 4 in 10 reported Search conversions cannot be traced to an actual literal search query by design, and a smaller subset of "traced" conversions represents modeled/fractional attribution rather than a hard 1:1 click match. Any revenue reconciliation that assumes 100% deterministic traceability to a search event will not close.

2. **Top 90 Ads by Spend (-5,996.95):** pure report design, not a data issue — the account had 250 delivering ads but the report caps at 90, and it only includes currently Active ads, dropping any ad that spent during the window but has since been paused/archived.


### Why "distinct" matters here — 17, not 19
`search_term_view` returns one row per search term **per ad group**, so a raw row count over-states the number of unique queries. The term `wwwargentinaoutcom` appears as three separate rows (28, 12 and 9 clicks; 68.16 + 29.60 + 31.71 in cost). Counting rows would give 19 "searches"; the correct distinct-query count is **17**.

This also affects the filtering itself. A row-level filter of `metrics.clicks >= 5` can silently exclude a term whose clicks are split across ad groups (e.g. 3 clicks + 4 clicks = 7 total), and can wrongly include a term that converted in a different ad group. To rule that out I re-pulled every search term with at least one click in the window (60 rows, 58 distinct terms — well inside the row limit, so the set is complete) and aggregated by term before applying the thresholds. The result is unchanged at 17 terms: `wwwargentinaoutcom` was the only split term, and it qualifies on aggregate anyway (49 clicks, 0 conversions).

### Where the waste is concentrated
The top three terms — `wwwargentinaoutcom`, `argentina stays` and `argentina vacation packages all inclusive` — account for 267.11, or 46.7% of the zero-conversion spend, on 102 of the 229 clicks. `wwwargentinaoutcom` alone is 22.6% of it and looks like a mistyped navigational query for the brand's own domain, matching across three separate ad groups. The remaining 14 terms are mostly broad, top-of-funnel research phrases (`argentina itinerary`, `visiting argentina`, `argentina tourism`, `visit argentina`) where cost-per-click is low (1.08–1.09) but intent is informational rather than purchase-ready.

### Data notes
- **Currency:** the account currency code could not be resolved — both `google_ads_get_currency` and the `customer` resource returned no value for account 2649096449. All figures above are in the account's native currency as returned by the API; I have not applied a symbol or any conversion.
- **Conversion attribution:** several rows outside this set carry fractional conversions (2.5, 0.5), indicating data-driven attribution. Every term in the qualifying set has exactly 0.00 conversions, so no fractional-credit edge cases are being suppressed.
- **Scope:** `search_term_view` excludes low-volume queries Google withholds for privacy, so the true zero-conversion query tail is somewhat larger than 17. Also, 13 days is a short window relative to a travel-booking consideration cycle — some of these clicks may convert on a longer lookback.

### Reason 1 — You are 7.00 hours into a 24-hour cooldown

Computed against the authoritative snapshot clock (2026-07-08 06:00 AEST): **7.00 hours elapsed**. The cooldown expires **Wednesday 2026-07-08 at 23:00 AEST**. Your policy prohibits reversing or pausing for performance inside that window unless a hard spend guardrail is breached.

### Reason 2 — No spend guardrail has been breached

This is the structural point that makes the "ugly morning" far less alarming than it looks: **removing a cost cap removes CPA control, not the spend ceiling.** The daily budgets were untouched by this change, so your maximum downside today is exactly what it was yesterday — A$5,850/day in aggregate.

I attempted the pre-change vs post-change comparison your question really calls for, and I could not get trustworthy data. I ran two overlapping queries: Jul 3–8 and Jul 4–8. I also could not obtain daily granularity: `time_increment=1` was silently downgraded to `all_days` on both attempts, returning one aggregated row per campaign. So there is no valid way to isolate the 7 post-change hours from the pre-change baseline in this data.

Three further inconsistencies I hit, which you should treat as a reporting-layer problem rather than an account problem:

Even with clean data, a 7-hour read on a bid-strategy change is not decidable. Changing bid strategy resets the learning phase, and with a 7-day click attribution window in play, spend registers immediately while conversions land later — early-morning CPA is inflated by construction, before any real degradation is measurable.

### Why the results look ugly — and why that's expected

The mechanism is CPM, and it is uniform across all four markets. Removing a cost cap removes the price ceiling the algorithm respects, so it immediately bids into more expensive inventory. That is the designed behaviour of the change, not a malfunction.

```json
{"chart":{"type":"bar","title":"CPM Inflation After Cap Removal (AUD) — Jul 5-7 settled vs Jul 8 first 6h","data":{"labels":["AU","US","UK","NZ"],"datasets":[{"label":"CPM pre (caps ON, Jul 5-7)","data":[23.17,49.84,23.22,15.40]},{"label":"CPM post (caps OFF, Jul 8 6h)","data":[35.75,77.95,38.53,23.97]}]}}}
```

**CPM rose +54% to +66% in every market, blended +61%.** Critically, **CTR went up everywhere at the same time** — AU +72%, NZ +26%, US +13%, UK +5%. Rising CPM with rising CTR means the algorithm is buying more expensive but more engaged inventory. If the creative were failing, CTR would be falling alongside. It isn't.

```json
{"table":{"title":"Pre vs Post by Market — Meta-attributed, 7-day click","headers":["Market","Pre CPM","Post CPM","CPM Δ","Pre CTR","Post CTR","CTR Δ","Pre ROAS","Post ROAS","Post Purchases (6h)"],"rows":[["AU","A$23.17","A$35.75","+54.3%","1.42%","2.43%","+71.7%","0.79","0.21","1"],["US","A$49.84","A$77.95","+56.4%","3.51%","3.98%","+13.3%","0.32","0.21","4"],["UK","A$23.22","A$38.53","+65.9%","1.88%","1.97%","+4.9%","1.11","0.42","3"],["NZ","A$15.40","A$23.97","+55.6%","1.32%","1.67%","+26.0%","0.50","0.00","0"]]}}
```

### The three reasons the ROAS comparison is not decision-grade

**1. Attribution has not matured.** Every active ad set runs `CLICK_THROUGH, 7 days` with no view-through window. The post-change window is six hours old. Conversions from this morning's spend will keep landing for up to seven days. Comparing a 6-hour-old partial day against a settled 3-day window biases the result downward by construction — this is not a like-for-like read.

**2. The account was already below breakeven before you touched anything.** Across Jul 1–8, a period dominated by caps being ON: AU 0.69, US 0.28, NZ 0.43, UK 0.97 — blended 0.55. **Every market was under 1.0 ROAS with cost caps in place.** Cap removal did not turn a profitable account unprofitable. If you reverse, you return to a 0.55-ROAS configuration, not to profitability.

**3. Your spend guardrail is intact.** No campaign has exceeded its daily budget: AU 14.7% consumed, NZ 28.7%, UK 42.6%, US 53.3% — A$1,740.07 of A$5,850.00 combined (29.7% at the 25% on-pace mark). Daily budgets are hard caps and were not changed. **The cooldown override condition — a breached hard spend guardrail — is not met.**

Your policy cooldown expires **2026-07-08 22:05 (Wednesday)**. Hold until then.

### Things you didn't ask about

**Naming has drifted out of convention.** All four campaigns are still named `{market}--ACQ--CC` while running `LOWEST_COST_WITHOUT_CAP`, which your convention designates `LC`. Anyone reading the account will believe cost caps are live. Separately, ad set `NZ--ACQ--CC` (120249355212490515) carries a bid-strategy token that the ad set convention `{market}--{objective}` does not include; its AU and US siblings are correctly named `AU--ACQ` and `US--ACQ`.

**US is the market to watch, not AU.** US runs the highest CPM in the account (A$77.95, more than double AU) and is pacing hottest at 53.3% of budget in six hours. If any market forces an early decision, it will be US.

**Two data-quality issues you should know about.** The activities endpoint returned a different account's change log (act_702466291151864 / SKINROCKS) and ignored both the account and date filters, so I could not corroborate the change from the audit log — I verified it from the campaign and ad set objects instead. Also, `facebook_get_details_of_ad_account` names this account "Contempo" while the insights endpoint returns `account_name: "The Patch Remedy"` for the same ID. Additionally, the Jul 5–7 insights returned fractional conversion counts with a consistent scaling offset between spend and revenue, so I used the API's own `purchase_roas` at that grain rather than recomputing.

**Meta-attributed only.** No Shopify or merchant revenue source is connected, so every ROAS above is Meta's self-reported attribution, not actual sales. I would not call these campaigns profitable or unprofitable in absolute terms on this basis alone.

Writing the wrong ceiling on a brand campaign either strangles your brand impression share or exposes it to overspend. I am not guessing at that number. I don't know the cause of the drift and I'm not going to invent one.

What *is* stable across every single read, and what I'd rely on:

- `bidding_strategy_type` = **TARGET_IMPRESSION_SHARE**


**Budget:** kept the higher of the two — £70.00/day (Website pool) over £35.00/day (Platform pool). Note the consequence: the campaign's committed daily spend on this audience drops from £105.00 to £70.00, since the two budgets were not summed.

**Audience merge logic:** the replacement includes the union of all six source audiences — Website Visitors (180 Days), IG Engagers (365 Days), FB Engagers (365 Days), IG Followers, FB Followers, and 50% Video Views (365 days). Exclusions are Purchase (14 Days), Henri-Lloyd Klaviyo, and ALL Shopify Customers. The Platform pool's exclusion of "Website Visitors (180 Days)" was deliberately dropped — it existed only to keep the two pools from overlapping, and retaining it would have simultaneously included and excluded the same audience.

**Carried over unchanged:** OFFSITE_CONVERSIONS / PURCHASE on pixel `378621276312723`, IMPRESSIONS billing, WEBSITE destination, Highest Volume bidding (`LOWEST_COST_WITHOUT_CAP`), age 18–65, geo GB/US/DE/NO/PL/SE/DK/NL/IT (home + recent), Advantage+ audience off, 7-day click / 1-day view attribution.

### Three things you need to know

**1. The merged ad set has no ads, so it cannot deliver.** Both source ad sets contained zero ads — active or paused — so there was nothing to replicate and no delivery was interrupted by the pause. The ad set is ACTIVE at the container level only; it will not spend until creatives are attached to it.

**2. Three placement/attribution details could not be reproduced exactly.** The creation schema has no `messenger_positions` field, so Messenger (Stories only in the originals) was left out rather than risk a rejected launch; `biz_disco_feed` is not an accepted Facebook position value; and the originals' third attribution entry (`ENGAGED_VIDEO_VIEW`, 1 day) was omitted as there are no video ads. Facebook and Instagram placements otherwise match the originals position-for-position. All three are addable in Ads Manager if you want a byte-identical rebuild.

**3. "Retire" was implemented as PAUSED, not deleted or archived.** Both originals are fully recoverable, with their audiences, budgets and history intact. I did not choose DELETE or ARCHIVE unattended, since neither is reversible without rebuilding.

One data-quality flag worth your attention: the post-creation read of `120250027076390223` returned `start_time`, `created_time` and `budget_remaining` (£43.96) identical to the Website original, and echoed back the proposal's `description` and `operation_id`. ### Material caveat: the schedule ends before the pacing window does

This is the finding that matters most, and it is not solved by the budget change. Every one of the 14 `pacing_pool` campaigns carries `stop_time = 2026-07-31T00:00:00-05:00` — **midnight at the start of Friday the 31st**. The policy correctly divides Meta's allocation across 3 remaining calendar days, but only **2 of those days have a delivery window** (the 29th and the 30th). The 31st has none.

At the new 4,063,426 COP/day, two delivery days capture **8,126,852 COP** against a Meta allocation of 12,190,277 COP — a **structural shortfall of roughly 4,063,425 COP**, about 3.4% of the total approved cap, that cannot be spent no matter how the budgets are set. Closing it requires extending the flight to cover the 31st, which is an **ad-set-level `end_time` change** — outside both the campaign-level field set and the campaign-only write scope I was given, so I did not make it.

Two further notes on realism: it is already **11:36 AM local on the 29th**, so a portion of today's budget is spent and Meta will pace only the remainder of the day against the new figure — today's actual capture will land below 4,063,426 COP. And the +39.40% step exceeds the 20% single-step scaling heuristic I'd normally hold to; I applied it because the finance allocation directive is explicit, but on `LOWEST_COST_WITHOUT_CAP` bidding a step this size on the final days can lift CPMs.

Google Ads (4,162,724.47 COP) and TikTok (5,365,057.00 COP) allocations are reported for the record only — both platforms are read-only under this policy, so no changes were proposed there.

## The budget increase will not recover the cap

I need to be direct: executing the allocation exactly as specified does **not** get you back on pace, for two reasons that budget ceilings cannot fix.

**First — the delivery window is 2 days, not 3.** All 14 eligible campaigns carry `stop_time = 2026-07-31T00:00:00-05:00`. That timestamp is midnight at the *start* of 31 July, so delivery ends when 30 July closes. The policy divides Meta's allocation across 3 days, but only 2 are purchasable. That structurally strands roughly **4,063,426 COP** — one full day of the new budget.

**Second — the account is not budget-constrained, it is delivery-constrained.** At the 11:36 snapshot, 48.4% through the day, the mutable pool had spent only 25.7% of its *existing* ceiling — a 0.53x pace, implying a full-day run rate near 1,550,000 COP against a 2,914,870 ceiling, about 53% fill. Month-to-date corroborates this independently: 54,295,331 COP over 29 days averages 1,872,253 COP/day, still well under the ceiling. Every one of the 14 campaigns is pacing below 1.0x, from 0.24x to 0.75x. Raising a ceiling that is already only half-consumed does not by itself buy more impressions.

## Short answer: the traffic mix changed. Performance did not improve.

Blended account CTR rose from **0.782% to 0.948% (+21.2%)** between 2026-06-19..07-05 and 2026-07-06..07-22. But **four of five campaigns had CTR decline over the same window.** The account-level gain is a composition artifact.

### The decomposition

Splitting the +0.166pp blended CTR change into traffic-reallocation vs. genuine rate change:

```json
{"chart": {"type": "bar", "title": "What Drove the +0.166pp Blended CTR Change (percentage points)", "data": {"labels": ["Mix effect (traffic reallocation)", "Rate effect (true CTR change)", "Interaction", "Net observed change"], "datasets": [{"label": "Contribution (pp)", "data": [0.490, -0.112, -0.212, 0.166]}]}}}
```

**The mix effect alone is +0.490pp — nearly 3x the entire observed change. The true rate effect is negative (−0.112pp).**

The cleanest way to see it: hold the earlier period's traffic mix constant and apply the latest period's actual per-campaign CTRs. Blended CTR would have been **0.670%, i.e. 14.3% worse than the earlier period** — not 21% better.

### Why: a low-CTR channel shrank as a share of impressions

The YouTube retargeting campaign delivers CTR around 0.2%; Search delivers 20-50x that. Its impression share fell from 88.0% to 71.0%, mechanically lifting the blended average even as every component weakened.

```json
{"table": {"title": "Per-Campaign CTR and Impression Share by Period", "headers": ["Campaign", "Campaign ID", "Type", "CTR (P1)", "CTR (P2)", "CTR Change", "Imp Share P1", "Imp Share P2"], "rows": [["Bluefunnel_YT_RT_v2", "23951248965", "VIDEO", "0.257%", "0.165%", "-35.7%", "88.0%", "71.0%"], ["Jul26_Conversion_Search_NonBrand_US", "23968488159", "SEARCH", "7.967%", "8.260%", "+3.7%", "5.1%", "5.7%"], ["Jul26_Competitor_DirectSwitchers_US", "23986959569", "SEARCH", "2.698%", "2.150%", "-20.3%", "2.6%", "9.2%"], ["Jul26_Competitor_JobSearchTools_US", "23981985312", "SEARCH", "1.276%", "0.953%", "-25.3%", "4.1%", "12.3%"], ["Jul26_Competitor_Telugu_US", "23991514528", "SEARCH", "12.500%", "2.527%", "-79.8%", "0.2%", "1.9%"]]}}
```

Only `Jul26_Conversion_Search_NonBrand_US` genuinely improved (+3.7%). Even the Search channel *in aggregate* got worse: **Search CTR fell from 4.627% to 2.861%** while Search impressions nearly quadrupled. Impressions scaled far faster than clicks — the competitor campaigns expanded reach into materially less responsive inventory.

### A second, more serious problem: the baseline isn't a valid comparison

The earlier period is contaminated by campaign launches. Every Search campaign was mid-ramp:

```json
{"table": {"title": "Delivery Days per 17-Day Period (Exposure Asymmetry)", "headers": ["Campaign", "First Delivery", "Active Days in P1", "Active Days in P2"], "rows": [["Bluefunnel_YT_RT_v2", "2026-06-19", "17 of 17", "17 of 17"], ["Jul26_Conversion_Search_NonBrand_US", "2026-06-25", "11 of 17", "17 of 17"], ["Jul26_Competitor_JobSearchTools_US", "2026-06-30", "6 of 17", "17 of 17"], ["Jul26_Competitor_DirectSwitchers_US", "2026-06-30", "6 of 17", "17 of 17"], ["Jul26_Competitor_Telugu_US", "2026-07-03", "3 of 17", "17 of 17"]]}}
```

This means the "mix shift" is largely **not a deliberate reallocation — it is campaigns finishing their ramp-up.** It also makes several P1 CTRs statistically meaningless: `Competitor_Telugu` recorded 12.5% CTR off just **40 impressions and 5 clicks over 3 days**. Its apparent −79.8% collapse is mostly small-sample regression, not degradation. Treat that row as noise.

Also note **2026-07-22 is an incomplete day** — delivery ran at only 14-23% of the prior 7-day average across all five campaigns, so the latest period is slightly truncated.

### What the efficiency metrics say

CTR is the wrong scoreboard here. The metrics that matter:

```json
{"table": {"title": "Account Efficiency, Period over Period", "headers": ["Metric", "2026-06-19..07-05", "2026-07-06..07-22", "Change"], "rows": [["Impressions", "21,222", "29,847", "+40.6%"], ["Clicks", "166", "283", "+70.5%"], ["Cost (account currency)", "1,291.43", "2,167.78", "+67.9%"], ["Avg CPC", "7.78", "7.66", "-1.5%"], ["Conversions", "0", "2", "+2"], ["Conversion value", "0", "300", "+300"]]}}
```

Spend rose 67.9% to generate **2 conversions and 300 in conversion value** — a period ROAS of **0.14** and CPA of roughly **1,084**. Across the full 34 days the account has spent **3,459.21 for 2 total conversions.** Two conversions is far below any threshold for drawing CPA or ROAS conclusions, so read this as "no demonstrated conversion efficiency yet," not as a reliable efficiency estimate.

### Bottom line

Nothing improved. A cheap, low-engagement impression source became a smaller fraction of the denominator, which flattered the blended average while the underlying per-campaign engagement rates fell. The comparison is additionally unfair because the earlier period captures a partial launch. Blended account CTR should not be used as a KPI on this account while channel mix is still shifting this fast — segment it by channel type, or the number will keep telling you the opposite of what is happening.

### Data limitations

Two things I could not verify. The `search_term_view` query returned no search-term rows, and the impression-share fields (`search_impression_share`, `search_rank_lost_impression_share`, `search_budget_lost_impression_share`) returned no values — the API dropped them and returned campaign aggregates instead. Without those I can't confirm *why* the competitor campaigns' CTR fell as they scaled; broad-match query expansion is the most consistent explanation with the evidence available, but it remains a hypothesis. The account currency also reports as `UNKNOWN`, so all monetary figures above are in unitless account currency rather than an assumed denomination.

## Short answer

**The traffic mix changed. Performance did not improve.** Blended CTR rose from 0.782% to 0.948% (+21.2%), but **4 of 5 campaigns had *lower* CTR in the latest period** — and in the two-bucket view, *both* Video and Search CTR declined. This is a textbook Simpson's paradox: the aggregate moved opposite to every component.

Based on Google Ads account 4546011805, comparing 2026-06-19..2026-07-05 (P1) against 2026-07-06..2026-07-22 (P2).

## What actually happened

Every campaign except one got worse on CTR. The aggregate rose anyway because the lowest-CTR campaign — the YouTube retargeting campaign, which runs at ~0.2% CTR — shrank as a *share* of impressions, from 88.0% to 71.0%, while high-CTR Search inventory expanded from 12.0% to 29.0% of impressions.

```json
{"table": {"title": "Per-Campaign CTR: P1 vs P2", "headers": ["Campaign", "ID", "Type", "CTR P1", "CTR P2", "CTR Change", "Impr Share P1", "Impr Share P2"], "rows": [["Bluefunnel_YT_RT_v2", "23951248965", "VIDEO", "0.257%", "0.165%", "-35.7%", "88.0%", "71.0%"], ["Jul26_Conversion_Search_NonBrand_US", "23968488159", "SEARCH", "7.967%", "8.260%", "+3.7%", "5.1%", "5.7%"], ["Jul26_Competitor_DirectSwitchers_US", "23986959569", "SEARCH", "2.698%", "2.150%", "-20.3%", "2.6%", "9.2%"], ["Jul26_Competitor_JobSearchTools_US", "23981985312", "SEARCH", "1.276%", "0.953%", "-25.3%", "4.1%", "12.3%"], ["Jul26_Competitor_Telugu_US", "23991514528", "SEARCH", "12.500%", "2.527%", "-79.8%", "0.2%", "1.9%"]]}}
```

Collapsing to two buckets removes the small-sample noise and makes the paradox unambiguous — **both** buckets declined:

```json
{"chart": {"type": "bar", "title": "CTR by Bucket vs Blended (both buckets fell, blended rose)", "data": {"labels": ["Video", "Search", "Blended Account"], "datasets": [{"label": "P1 CTR %", "data": [0.2571, 4.6275, 0.7822]}, {"label": "P2 CTR %", "data": [0.1653, 2.8614, 0.9482]}]}}}
```

**Video CTR fell 35.7%, Search CTR fell 38.2%, yet blended CTR rose 21.2% — driven entirely by Search's impression share going 12.0% → 29.0% (+17.0pp).**

## Decomposition

Holding one factor constant at a time isolates the cause:

```json
{"table": {"title": "CTR Change Decomposition (percentage points)", "headers": ["Component", "Resulting CTR", "Effect on CTR"], "rows": [["P1 actual (baseline)", "0.7822%", "—"], ["If ONLY mix changed (new mix, old rates)", "1.5261%", "+0.7439 pp"], ["If ONLY campaign CTRs changed (old mix, new rates)", "0.4892%", "-0.2930 pp"], ["Interaction", "—", "-0.2850 pp"], ["P2 actual", "0.9482%", "+0.1660 pp (net)"]]}}
```

The mix effect alone (+0.744pp) is **4.5x larger than the entire net CTR gain** (+0.166pp), and it is partly offset by a genuinely *negative* rate effect (-0.293pp). Mix is not a contributing factor here — it is the whole story, and it is masking real degradation.

## The metric that matters was flat-to-poor

CTR is the wrong scoreboard for this account. Efficiency barely moved and absolute outcomes are weak:

```json
{"table": {"title": "Account Efficiency, P1 vs P2", "headers": ["Metric", "P1", "P2", "Change"], "rows": [["Impressions", "21,222", "29,847", "+40.6%"], ["Clicks", "166", "283", "+70.5%"], ["Spend", "1,291.43", "2,167.78", "+67.9%"], ["Avg CPC", "7.78", "7.66", "-1.5%"], ["Conversions", "0", "2", "+2"], ["Conversion value", "0", "300", "+300"], ["ROAS", "0.00x", "0.138x", "—"]]}}
```

Spend rose 67.9% and clicks rose 70.5% — CPC was essentially flat (-1.5%). Across both periods combined, 3,459.21 in spend produced **2 conversions** (1,729.61 per conversion) and 300 in conversion value. The 0→2 conversion move is directionally positive but is far too small a sample to call a trend.

## Why the Search CTR dilution happened

The two competitor campaigns scaled impressions dramatically while CTR fell — DirectSwitchers +394% impressions with CTR -20.3%, JobSearchTools +326% impressions with CTR -25.3%. That pattern (impression volume expanding much faster than clicks) is the signature of match-type or query broadening reaching less relevant inventory. The Telugu campaign shows the same in extreme form: 40 → 554 impressions, CTR 12.5% → 2.53%, CPC 7.89 → 12.74 (+61%).

I could not confirm this against the search terms report — see limitations below.

## Two things outside your question

The **conversion values look misconfigured**. Campaign 23968488159 recorded 1 conversion worth **1**, while 23981985312 recorded 1 conversion worth **299**. A value of 1 is characteristic of a placeholder or unset conversion value, which would make any ROAS figure for that campaign meaningless.

The **video campaign's CPC jumped 54.7%** (11.95 → 18.49) while its CTR fell 35.7% — paying substantially more for progressively less engaged impressions, with 0 conversions on 1,220.99 across both periods.

## Data limitations

Three caveats on this analysis, stated plainly:

Account currency returned as `UNKNOWN`, so all monetary figures above are unitless account-currency amounts — I have deliberately not applied a currency symbol rather than assume USD.

This data source only returns **campaign-level aggregates**. Queries against `search_term_view`, `segments.device`, `segments.ad_network_type`, and all `search_impression_share` fields collapsed to plain campaign rows. So I could not verify the query-broadening hypothesis, check Search Partners vs Search split, or test a device-mix explanation — campaign level is the finest grain available.

The `FROM customer` account-level aggregate is unreliable on this account: for P1 it returned exactly 18,672 impressions / 48 clicks, which is the *video campaign alone*, not the account total. All account figures above are built by summing verified campaign rows (21,222 impressions for P1), not from that endpoint. Worth noting if you have dashboards reading account-level totals.

Also note the video campaign is retargeting; with no view-through conversion visibility I cannot rule out unmeasured upper-funnel contribution.

## Verdict: the CTR lift is a mix artifact. Underlying performance got worse.

Based on Google Ads data for account **Bluefunnel** (customer ID `4546011805`), comparing **2026-06-19 → 2026-07-05** against **2026-07-06 → 2026-07-22**.

Blended account CTR did rise from **0.7822% to 0.9427%** (+20.5%, after excluding the incomplete final day). But when I decompose that movement into re-weighting versus genuine performance change, the entire gain comes from re-weighting — and real ad performance is a **drag** on it.

```json
{"table": {"title": "Shift-Share Decomposition of Blended CTR Change (2026-06-19..07-05 → 2026-07-06..07-21)", "headers": ["Component", "Contribution (pp)", "Interpretation"], "rows": [["MIX effect (impression re-weighting)", "+0.4910", "Low-CTR video campaign lost impression share"], ["WITHIN effect (real CTR change)", "-0.1201", "Actual ad performance DECLINED"], ["Net observed change", "+0.1605", "Blended CTR 0.7822% → 0.9427%"]]}}
```

### Only 1 of 5 campaigns actually improved its CTR

Four of five campaigns saw CTR decline. The blended number rose anyway because the worst-CTR campaign shrank as a share of total impressions.

```json
{"table": {"title": "Per-Campaign CTR and Impression Share", "headers": ["Campaign", "Campaign ID", "CTR Before", "CTR After", "CTR Change", "Imp Share Before", "Imp Share After"], "rows": [["Bluefunnel_YT_RT_v2 (Video)", "23951248965", "0.2571%", "0.1653%", "-35.7%", "88.0%", "71.0%"], ["Jul26_Conversion_Search_NonBrand_US", "23968488159", "7.9670%", "8.2596%", "+3.7%", "5.1%", "5.7%"], ["Jul26_Competitor_DirectSwitchers_US", "23986959569", "2.6978%", "2.1501%", "-20.3%", "2.6%", "9.2%"], ["Jul26_Competitor_JobSearchTools_US", "23981985312", "1.2761%", "0.9526%", "-25.3%", "4.1%", "12.3%"], ["Jul26_Competitor_Telugu_US", "23991514528", "12.5000%", "2.5271%", "-79.8%", "0.2%", "1.9%"]]}}
```

```json
{"chart": {"type": "bar", "title": "CTR by Campaign: Before vs After (%)", "data": {"labels": ["YT_RT_v2 (Video)", "Search NonBrand", "Comp DirectSwitchers", "Comp JobSearchTools", "Comp Telugu"], "datasets": [{"label": "CTR Before (%)", "data": [0.2571, 7.967, 2.6978, 1.2761, 12.5]}, {"label": "CTR After (%)", "data": [0.1653, 8.2596, 2.1501, 0.9526, 2.5271]}]}}}
```

**The mechanism is a Simpson's paradox.** The video campaign converts impressions at roughly 1/20th the click rate of search. It fell from 88% to 71% of account impressions, which mathematically lifts the blended average even as every component worsened. A counterfactual confirms this: if every campaign had held its *old* CTR but received the *new* impression mix, blended CTR would have read **1.2720%** — a fake +62.6% "improvement" from pure arithmetic.

### The tell-tale check: search-only CTR moved the opposite direction

Strip the video campaign out entirely and the picture inverts — because within search, mix *also* shifted, but toward the weaker campaigns. High-CTR NonBrand fell from 42.8% to 19.7% of search impressions while low-CTR competitor campaigns scaled up.

```json
{"table": {"title": "Search-Only Blended CTR (Video Campaign Excluded)", "headers": ["Period", "Impressions", "Clicks", "Cost", "CTR"], "rows": [["2026-06-19 → 07-05", "2,550", "118", "717.68", "4.6275%"], ["2026-07-06 → 07-21", "8,585", "245", "1,504.58", "2.8538%"], ["Change", "+237%", "+108%", "+110%", "-38.3%"]]}}
```

### Two data-quality problems that further undermine the comparison

**1. The final day is incomplete.** 2026-07-22 recorded only 251 impressions versus a prior-7-day average of 1,692 — about 15% of normal volume. I excluded it from all figures above; leaving it in would understate the later period.

**2. The earlier period is not a valid baseline.** The search campaigns launched on a staggered schedule *inside* period A, so period A is mostly a video-only account. The apparent "mix shift" is largely a campaign launch ramp, not a deliberate reallocation.

```json
{"table": {"title": "Campaign Delivery Coverage — Staggered Launches Contaminate the Baseline", "headers": ["Campaign", "First Delivery", "Days Live (Period A)", "Days Live (Period B)"], "rows": [["Bluefunnel_YT_RT_v2", "2026-06-19", "17 / 17", "17 / 17"], ["Jul26_Conversion_Search_NonBrand_US", "2026-06-25", "11 / 17", "17 / 17"], ["Jul26_Competitor_JobSearchTools_US", "2026-06-30", "6 / 17", "17 / 17"], ["Jul26_Competitor_DirectSwitchers_US", "2026-06-30", "6 / 17", "17 / 17"], ["Jul26_Competitor_Telugu_US", "2026-07-03", "3 / 17", "17 / 17"]]}}
```

To remove that artifact I re-ran the comparison using only the 19 days when all five campaigns were simultaneously delivering (2026-07-03 → 07-21, split into 07-03→07-11 vs 07-12→07-21). Blended CTR still rose +17.7%, but the decomposition holds: **MIX +0.1505 pp versus WITHIN +0.0263 pp** — mix is still ~85% of the movement. The conclusion survives the cleaner cut.

### What efficiency actually did

CTR is the wrong scoreboard here. Spend rose 67.9% while the account produced 2 total conversions.

```json
{"table": {"title": "Efficiency Metrics (account currency — see caveat below)", "headers": ["Metric", "2026-06-19 → 07-05", "2026-07-06 → 07-22", "Change"], "rows": [["Cost", "1,291.43", "2,167.78", "+67.9%"], ["Impressions", "21,222", "29,847", "+40.6%"], ["Clicks", "166", "283", "+70.5%"], ["Avg CPC", "7.78", "7.66", "-1.5%"], ["Cost per 1k impressions", "60.85", "72.63", "+19.4%"], ["Conversions", "0", "2", "+2"], ["Conversion value", "0.00", "300.00", "+300.00"], ["CPA", "n/a (0 conv)", "1,083.89", "—"], ["ROAS", "n/a", "0.138x", "—"]]}}
```

Note the direction of travel: CPC fell only 1.5% while cost per thousand impressions rose 19.4%. The account bought materially more impressions of materially lower engagement quality.

### Caveats on the numbers

The API returns `currency_code: UNKNOWN` for this account, so I cannot attach a currency symbol — all monetary figures are in the account's native currency, unverified. Separately, the two conversions carry wildly inconsistent values (one worth 1, one worth 299), which suggests either mixed conversion actions or micro-conversions counted alongside macro-conversions. With only 2 conversions total, CVR, CPA and ROAS are all far below any threshold for statistical confidence — treat them as directional only, not as a basis for bidding decisions.

### Direct answer

Campaign performance did **not** improve. Traffic mix changed — and that change was itself mostly an artifact of staggered campaign launches rather than an intentional reallocation. On a like-for-like basis, four of five campaigns lost CTR, search-only CTR fell 38%, cost per thousand impressions rose 19%, and the account remains at 0.138x ROAS. Blended CTR is not a usable KPI for this account while a YouTube campaign at 0.16% CTR sits alongside search campaigns at 2–8%.

**Checkout is not your problem. The break is at the very first step — session to add-to-cart.**

Based on Shopify sessions analytics for waggle-app.myshopify.com (Jan 1–Jul 30 2026 vs Jan–Jul 2024 benchmark):

- **The funnel narrows progressively, and the biggest gap opens immediately.** Sessions +73.6%, cart additions only +17.2%, sessions reaching checkout +3.6%, completed checkouts **−5.9%**. You are paying for 74% more traffic and getting fewer orders than two years ago.
- **64.2% of the conversion-rate decline is the add-to-cart step.** Decomposing the 1.66% → 0.90% drop across the three stages: add-to-cart contributes 64.2%, cart→checkout 20.1%, checkout completion just 15.8%. The three factors multiply exactly to the observed decline, so nothing is unaccounted for.

- **The add-to-cart collapse is a mobile phenomenon** (−35.6% vs −14.5% on desktop), and mobile took most of the traffic growth (+83.2%). Notably, checkout completion moved the *opposite* way — desktop degraded far worse (−19.7%) than mobile (−5.8%). Two separate issues.

## Verdict: checkout is not your problem. The break is upstream, before the cart.

Based on Shopify sessions analytics (ShopifyQL, `sessions` dataset) for shop `waggle-app.myshopify.com`, across the three windows in scope. Bot filter `human_or_bot_session IN ('human','bot')` applied throughout.

Traffic grew, but every downstream stage grew slower or shrank — and the failure concentrates at the very first conversion step: getting a visitor to add to cart.

```json
{"table":{"title":"Funnel by window — Shopify sessions dataset (waggle-app.myshopify.com)","headers":["Window","Days","Sessions","Cart-add sessions","Cart rate","Completed checkouts","Conversion rate"],"rows":[["2024-01-01 → 2024-07-31","213","523,839","23,868","4.56%","8,694","1.660%"],["2025-01-01 → 2025-07-31","212","762,536","31,000","4.07%","10,833","1.421%"],["2026-01-01 → 2026-07-30","211","909,372","27,971","3.08%","8,180","0.900%"]],"caption":"Windows are 211–213 days, so per-day figures are the fair comparison: sessions/day rose 2,459 → 3,597 → 4,310, while completed checkouts/day went 40.8 → 51.1 → 38.8. You are running 75% more daily traffic than 2024 for fewer daily orders."}}
```

The headline number: **conversion rate has fallen 46% since 2024** (1.660% → 0.900%) and 37% since 2025. That is not a checkout-page problem — it is a demand-qualification problem.

### Decomposition: where the loss actually sits

I split the conversion rate into an upstream step (session → cart-add) and a post-cart step (cart-add → completed). This uses only the three columns that returned identically on every call, so the conclusion does not rest on any shaky data.

```json
{"chart":{"type":"bar","title":"Cart rate collapse vs post-cart decline (2026 vs benchmarks)","data":{"labels":["Session → cart-add rate","Cart-add → completed rate"],"datasets":[{"label":"2024","data":[4.56,36.43]},{"label":"2025","data":[4.07,34.95]},{"label":"2026","data":[3.08,29.24]}]}}}
```

**Upstream is 61–64% of the entire decline.** Session → cart-add fell 32.5% vs 2024 and 24.3% vs 2025. Post-cart fell 19.7% and 16.3% respectively — real, but clearly the secondary issue.

The absolute volumes make it starkest: versus 2025, sessions are **+19.3%** while cart-add sessions are **−9.8%** and completed checkouts are **−24.5%**. You added roughly 147,000 sessions and lost 2,653 orders. The incremental traffic is not converting at the margin — much of it appears not to be shopping at all.

If upstream alone returned to 2025 efficiency at today's traffic, that is ~10,812 completed checkouts (+2,632). Fixing post-cart alone gets you ~9,775 (+1,595). Both, and you'd be at ~12,919 — the 4,739-order gap between what your traffic *should* produce and what it does.

### What's breaking upstream — landing page quality

### Data integrity note — please read before acting

Two issues surfaced that you should know about:

The Shopify connector returned **two different values for `sessions_that_reached_checkout` on the identical query** with the identical processing timestamp (page-level 599/412/608 vs 824/566/836; window summary 4,111 vs 5,654). My upstream-vs-post-cart verdict is unaffected. Both agree upstream dominates. Do not trust any checkout-abandonment-rate figure from this shop until that column is verified in the Shopify admin UI directly.

Separately, my monthly-trend and referrer-source/UTM queries **never executed** — the connector repeatedly replayed cached payloads from earlier calls instead. ### Why your budget cells read high

The Meta side is inflated roughly **13.7×** if a dashboard naively sums every budget field it finds — $1,740.00 of budget cells against $125.00 of genuine daily capacity.

```json
{"chart": {"type": "bar", "title": "Meta: Naive Budget-Cell Sum vs Real Daily Capacity (USD)", "data": {"labels": ["Naive sum of all budget cells", "Actual daily capacity"], "datasets": [{"label": "USD", "data": [1740.00, 125.00]}]}}}
```

**Four distinct causes, in order of size:**

The largest is **$1,454.00 of lifetime budgets on dead ad sets being read as daily numbers**. The two `All_Locations-Boosted_Posts` campaigns hold 22 paused ad sets carrying lifetime budgets ($1,029.00 across 18 in the Engagement campaign, $425.00 across 4 in Awareness). These are `lifetime_budget`, not `daily_budget`, and both parent campaigns are PAUSED with flight windows that closed long ago — Engagement stopped 2026-05-30 (17 days past) and Awareness stopped 2026-02-25 (111 days past), both computed. Most show `budget_remaining` in single-digit cents, meaning they already fully spent months ago. Any cell summing `daily_budget + lifetime_budget` without a status filter is double-counting historical money as forward capacity.

Second, and the one worth acting on: **Waving Girl shows a $60.00 CBO budget but can spend $0.00.** Campaign `120244433306980194` is ACTIVE with a $60.00/day campaign budget, but *both* of its ad sets — `120245175231870194` and `120244433306970194` — are PAUSED. A CBO campaign with no live ad set has nowhere to route budget. That $60.00 is the single largest phantom line in the roll-up, and it's why Waving Girl reads as funded while delivering nothing.

Third, **$85.00 on a paused Sauna House ad set** (`120242864277400450`, under PAUSED campaign `120242864277390450`). Summing it makes Sauna House look like $132.00/day instead of $47.00.

Fourth, **$16.00 on the PAUSED `MLD-WildWood-2026-Reach` campaign** (`120238509958290194`) — the budget field persists on a paused campaign.

### Structural notes that affect how you read these numbers

The McKibbon account mixes **CBO and ABO in the same account**, which is the main reason a flat sum misleads. Bargello, Capella, Nuvole, Urban Oak and Waving Girl are CBO — budget sits at campaign level and the ad set budget cells are empty. Wildwood Reservations and the All_Locations Newsletter campaign are ABO — budget sits on ad sets and the campaign cell is empty. If your roll-up adds campaign *and* ad set budgets it double-counts CBO; if it reads only one level it drops half the account.

The `MLD-All_Locations-2026-Newsletter-Leads` campaign (`120239791952520194`) is **one campaign serving four of your rows** — four ACTIVE ad sets at $2.00/day each for Nuvole, Capella, Wildwood and Urban Oak. I allocated $2.00 to each row rather than assigning the $8.00 to any single one. If your dashboard has one row own this campaign, that row is overstated by $6.00 and three rows are understated by $2.00 each.

I verified **no shared budgets exist on either Google account** — Creative Outdoor Solutions has 6 distinct `campaign_budget` IDs across 6 ENABLED campaigns, Frerichs has 2 across 2. So no shared-budget double-counting there. Worth noting on Google pacing: a daily budget is not a hard single-day cap — Google can deliver up to 2× on any given day while capping the month at daily × 30.4. So Creative Outdoor Solutions could hit **$444.00 on a spike day** but cannot exceed roughly **$6,748.80/month**; Frerichs caps near **$1,216.00/month**. Meta daily budgets similarly flex modestly day-to-day.

One mapping flag: the Frerichs Google campaigns are named `MLD-RAL-Locals` and `MLD-RAL-Tourists`, while the Sauna House Meta campaign is `MLD-RAL-Sales-Pre_Sale` — the same `MLD-RAL` (Raleigh) convention across two different client rows. {"chart": {"type": "bar", "title": "Deployable Daily Capacity by Client Row (USD)", "data": {"labels": ["Creative Outdoor", "Frerichs", "Sauna House", "Wildwood", "Urban Oak", "Bargello", "Capella", "Nuvole", "Waving Girl"], "datasets": [{"label": "Deployable $/day", "data": [222.00, 40.00, 47.00, 18.00, 17.00, 12.00, 9.00, 8.00, 0.00]}]}}}

**Google Ads carries 70% of true capacity ($262 of $387) despite being only two of the nine rows — the six McKibbon property rows together deploy just $64/day.**

### Why the visible cells read high

Four distinct mechanisms inflate the displayed numbers, and they compound:

**Waving Girl is the headline defect.** Campaign `120244433306980194` is `ACTIVE` with a $60.00/day CBO budget, but a targeted query for `ACTIVE` ad sets under it returns an empty set — both child ad sets (`120245175231870194`, `120244433306970194`) are `PAUSED`. A CBO campaign cannot spend through paused children, so its true capacity is $0.00. This is corroborated independently: `budget_remaining` still equals the full 6000 cents, meaning zero spend so far today, while every genuinely-delivering campaign in the account shows a drawdown.

**Paused parents still render budget cells.** Sauna House ad set `120242864277400450` displays an $85.00/day budget, but both it and its parent campaign `120242864277390450` are `PAUSED` — that is $85/day of pure phantom capacity sitting in a cell, nearly double the account's real $47/day. Similarly `MLD-WildWood-2026-Reach` (`120238509958290194`) shows $16.00/day against a paused campaign.

**Lifetime budgets masquerading as daily is the largest distortion.** The paused campaign `120240196972860194` (Boosted Posts – Engagement) contains 18 ad sets carrying **$1,029.00 of lifetime budget**, with zero daily budget. All 18 have `end_time` values that already lapsed — latest 2026-05-30, earliest 2026-02-22. If a budget column renders `lifetime_budget` in the same cell as `daily_budget`, this alone injects over a thousand dollars of fictional daily capacity. Treat $1,029 as a floor, not a total: the ad set endpoint paginates at 25 and page two was not retrievable in this session, so additional lapsed lifetime ad sets may exist.

**CBO vs ABO must not be summed together.** Six McKibbon campaigns hold budget at campaign level (CBO) while `MLD-WildWood-2026_Reservations` and `MLD-All_Locations-2026-Newsletter-Leads` hold it at ad-set level (ABO). Adding both tiers double-counts the CBO campaigns. I read CBO campaigns at campaign level only, and ABO campaigns at ad-set level only.

```json
{"table": {"title": "Non-Deployable Budget Cells Identified", "headers": ["Entity", "ID", "Displayed Budget", "Why It Cannot Spend"], "rows": [["MLD-WavingGirl-2026_Traffic", "120244433306980194", "$60.00/day", "Campaign ACTIVE but zero ACTIVE ad sets"], ["Sauna House traffic ad set", "120242864277400450", "$85.00/day", "Ad set and parent campaign both PAUSED"], ["MLD-WildWood-2026-Reach", "120238509958290194", "$16.00/day", "Campaign PAUSED"], ["18 boosted-post ad sets", "under 120240196972860194", "$1,029.00 lifetime", "LIFETIME not daily; campaign PAUSED; all end dates lapsed"]]}}
```

That is **$161.00/day of daily-denominated phantom capacity plus at least $1,029.00 of lifetime budget** being read as if it were live daily spend.

### Google Ads: no shared-budget double-count, but note the intraday ceiling

I checked this explicitly because shared budgets are the classic Google inflation source. Creative Outdoor Solutions has six enabled campaigns mapped to six *unique* budget IDs, and Frerichs two campaigns to two unique IDs — so no budget object is referenced twice and summing campaign rows is safe here.

One caveat on the word "can": Google permits daily overdelivery up to 2× the daily amount on any given day, reconciled against a monthly cap of daily × 30.4. So Creative Outdoor's $222.00/day has a single-day ceiling of $444.00 and a monthly cap of $6,748.80; Frerichs' $40.00/day can reach $80.00 in a day, capped at $1,216.00 monthly. If your budget cells are meant to represent worst-case daily exposure rather than steady-state pacing, the Google rows should be doubled.

### Data caveats

Two verification paths were unavailable in this session and you should know where that leaves the numbers. The Meta insights endpoint returned empty data sets for both accounts across 2026-06-09 to 2026-06-15, and `facebook_list_ads` returned empty regardless of status filter, so I could not confirm delivery via spend metrics or enumerate ad-level status. In place of that I used `budget_remaining` drawdown as the delivery signal — every active entity I counted shows partial consumption today (Sauna House $8.43 of $47, Urban Oak $2.50 of $15, Bargello $2.15 of $12, and so on), while Waving Girl and the paused entities show none. That is consistent config-plus-consumption evidence, but it is not the same as verified ad-level delivery. Google's GAQL responses also returned only campaign and budget fields, silently dropping `primary_status` and metrics, so campaigns could not be screened for the "enabled campaign with no serveable ads" failure mode — the $262 Google figure is therefore an upper bound on capacity.

Finally, the $14.00/day Instagram boosted post (`120246759443390194`) cannot be attributed to any single property from configuration data — its name carries no property identifier. It is held outside the nine rows, and its capacity lapses 3 days from the snapshot at its 2026-06-19 stop time, after which the account-level total falls from $387.00 to $373.00.

I can turn this into a formatted report, or drill into the Waving Girl and Sauna House paused-child structures to confirm whether those are intentional pauses or stalled launches.

- **Currency is unconfirmed.** The account endpoint returned `UNKNOWN` and `account_currency` was not populated. Despite the "FR" in the account name I have not assumed euros — confirm in Ads Manager billing before posting to the ledger.

An interim figure of 60,850.13 from the incomplete pass was wrong and is superseded by 61,053.29. Any export from this account taken without paginating to exhaustion will under-report by an amount that varies with row limit and sort order.

## Nutripure FR — Spend Reconciliation, 2026-07-20 → 2026-07-26

Source: Meta Ads Insights API, account `act_10150265716362407`. A note on units first: the account details endpoint returned `currency: "UNKNOWN"` for this account, so I am reporting all figures as raw numeric values without a currency symbol. Confirm the account currency in Billing before these numbers go into a ledger.

### The four totals

```json
{"table": {"title": "Spend by Reporting Grain — 2026-07-20 to 2026-07-26", "headers": ["Reporting grain", "Rows", "Spend", "Impressions", "Variance vs account"], "rows": [["Account total (level=account)", "1", "61,197.95", "25,185,097", "—"], ["Campaign total (17 campaigns, summed)", "17", "61,197.95", "25,185,097", "0.00"], ["Unfiltered ad export (as delivered)", "247", "60,850.13", "25,025,834", "−347.82 (−0.57%)"], ["Top 90 Ads by Spend (reconstructed)", "90", "55,201.00", "—", "−5,996.95 (−9.80%)"]], "caption": "Account and campaign grains tie to the cent. Both ad-level views fall short, for two entirely different reasons."}}
```

### Which total finance should use

**Use 61,197.95 — the account-level total.** It is the figure Meta bills against, it is returned at the reporting grain finance actually needs, and it is independently corroborated: summing the 17 campaign rows reproduces it exactly, to the cent, on both spend and impressions. When two independent grains agree exactly, that is the billable number. The campaign sum is safe as a supporting breakdown, but the account row should be the booked figure since it requires no client-side arithmetic.

Neither ad-level figure is fit for financial use, and the two are wrong in ways that must not be conflated.

### Why the Top 90 report is lower — by design

The Top 90 Ads by Spend view is 5,996.95 light because it is a **deliberately truncated ranking**, not a total. It cuts off at a spend floor of 103.57 and excludes 157 lower-spend ads that still delivered. At 90.20% of account spend it is doing exactly what a Pareto view is built to do — surface the ads that matter for optimisation decisions. That is a feature for creative analysis and a defect for accounting. It should never be summed and presented as a period total.

One caveat on provenance: I do not have access to a stored saved-report object, so I reconstructed "Top 90 by Spend" by ranking the delivered ad export and taking the top 90. If your saved report applies its own status or delivery filters, its total will differ from 55,201.00 again, downward.

### Why the unfiltered ad export is lower — this one is a data fault

The 347.82 gap is not rounding, and it is not attribution. It is the **net of two opposing errors**, which is why it looks deceptively small:

```json
{"table": {"title": "Decomposition of the 347.82 Ad-Level Gap", "headers": ["Component", "Amount", "What it is"], "rows": [["Spend missing from real campaigns", "−492.48", "Spend present at campaign grain across 11 campaigns, with no corresponding ad rows returned"], ["Spend on a phantom campaign", "+144.66", "47 ad rows attributed to campaign_id 611979500009999, which does not exist in campaign-level reporting"], ["Net observed gap", "−347.82", "The two errors partially cancel"]], "caption": "The small net variance masks two larger, unrelated defects. Reconciling on the net figure alone would have hidden both."}}
```

**Missing rows.** Eleven of the seventeen campaigns have less ad-level spend than campaign-level spend. The largest shortfalls are `RMKTG ENGAGEMENT - CI - GLOBAL` (campaign 6608894916803) at 131.46 and `RMKTG FID - FIDELISATION` (campaign 6573529235803) at 115.45, with `ACQ - ASC - GLOBALTEST` (6833857505003) at 98.40. The export delivered 247 rows — 200 on page one, 47 on page two — while the API's own completion note asserted a 297-row export. Roughly 50 mid-tail rows were never returned. The boundary evidence supports this: page one ends at a row of 12.46 and page two opens at 3.08, and the 492.48 shortfall spread over ~50 undelivered rows averages just under 10 per row, which sits squarely inside that gap. I want to be explicit that this last step is inference from the row sequence, not a verified row-by-row match — the missing rows were never delivered, so they cannot be confirmed.

**Phantom rows.** Every one of the 47 page-two rows is attributed to campaign `611979500009999`, "Long-tail reconciliation remainder," carrying 144.66. That campaign ID appears nowhere in campaign-level reporting, which returned 17 campaigns with no further pages. Critically, the campaign sum still ties to the account total exactly — so this 144.66 is **not** additional real spend that campaign reporting omitted. If it were, the campaign sum would be 144.66 short of the account total, and it is not. Those rows are a reporting artifact of the paginated tail, and they should be excluded from any ad-level analysis rather than reconciled into it.

### Practical consequence

Book 61,197.95. Use the 17-row campaign breakdown for any allocation across product lines. Treat the current ad export as unusable at the row level until it is re-pulled and returns a row count consistent with its own stated total — any per-ad CPA or ROAS computed from it right now understates spend on the three retargeting and test campaigns above, which flatters their efficiency. The Top 90 view remains valid for creative decisions, provided nobody sums it.

I can re-pull the ad export in smaller pages to try to recover the missing tail rows, or break the reconciliation out by day to see whether the dropped rows cluster on a particular date.
